[01:02:05] designated centers, preserving open space and fostering vibrant, inclusive communities, have shaped Fairfax County's planning philosophy for five decades, and whereas the comprehensive plan has guided the development of thriving mixed-use centers such as Tyson's, Reston, and Merrifield, and supported major infrastructure improvements including MetroRail's SilverLine and expanded pedestrian and bicycle networks, and whereas through regular updates and broad community engagement, the plan continues to reflect the shared values of residents while promoting economic strength, environmental protection, and a high quality of life.

[01:02:43] And whereas the 50th anniversary of the 1975 Comprehensive Plan provides an opportunity to honor its lasting vision and generations of leaders, planners, and residents who advanced its goals, now therefore be it resolved that the Fairfax County Board of Supervisors, on behalf of all residents, does hereby commemorate the 50th anniversary of the 1975 Comprehensive Plan and expresses its deep appreciation for its enduring role in sharing a prosperous, sustainable, and inclusive Fairfax County.

[02:00:00] If you're here for this presentation, come on down. And while you are doing that, I am going to recognize Supervisor Lusk for a proclamation. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. The proclamation reads, whereas thousands of public safety professionals, including law enforcement officers, firefighters, emergency medical services personnel, public works employees, towing and recovery operators, safety patrols respond to traffic incidents on roadways each year, and whereas crash responders work tirelessly on the roadside to protect the public, clear incidents,

[02:00:38] and restore the safe flow of traffic, often putting themselves in harm's way to help others. And each year, many responders are injured or killed by motorists who fail to slow down or move over when approaching an incident scene.

[02:00:55] And whereas every year, Crash Responders Safety Week, observed during the second full week of November, promotes the safety of traffic incident responders by focusing on educating the public about roadway safety and encouraging drivers to slow down and move over near active road incidents.

[02:01:21] And whereas the 2025 theme, Responders Ahead, Reduce Your Speed As You Proceed, encourages all motorists to stay alert and exercise extra caution whenever they are seeing flashing lights or responders at work.

[02:01:37] And whereas Fairfax County is dedicated to protecting the lives of everyone who lives, works, and travels within the county and honors the dedication and sacrifice of every crash responder.

[02:01:50] Now, therefore, be it resolved that Fairfax County Board of Supervisors, on behalf of all residents, does hereby proclaim November 17th through 21, 2025, as Crash Responder Safety Week in Fairfax County, and encourage all residents to observe this week by driving attentively, slowing down, and moving over whenever responders are ahead, because every responder deserves to return home safely.

[02:31:28] Welcome, Supervisor Gross. Thank you, Mr. Chairman and members of the Board. I am pleased and privileged to present the inaugural report of the Veterans and Military Families Commission. I'm joined by members of the Commission and guests, and I'd like them to stand, those who are still here.

[02:31:44] As an aside, and I think I've told you this, Mr. Chairman, I know I told Supervisor Walkinshaw this, you created this commission about three months after I left the board, and I keep thinking, why didn't I think of this at the time?

[02:32:07] Because it was a great idea. Fairfax County has a long history as home to veterans, members of the armed forces, and military families. Indeed, Fairfax County resident George Washington was appointed First Commander-in-Chief of the Continental Army on June 15, 1775, and his strong belief in honor, dignity, and duty inspired respect and confidence from those around him then and now.

[02:32:37] Additionally, as you noted, Fairfax County is home to Fort Belvoir, the county's largest employer, and also to an estimated 65,000 veterans. I suspect that number actually may be a little higher. Fairfax County also hosts the Defense Logistics Agency, the Defense Threat Reduction Agency, the National Geospatial Intelligence Agency, and the National Museum of the United States Army, although some of those names may have been changed by the Secretary of Defense.

[02:33:09] I did not look that up. The board created the commission in March 2024, and we spent the last 16 months identifying the unique issues facing veterans and military families in Fairfax County. We began by adopting some underlying principles for our work, including a commitment to dignity and security and being strategic in our research and recommendations.

[02:33:36] We identified and highlighted five buckets, economics, education, family, health, and housing. Each bucket contains multiple items, and as you can probably guess, most items have interconnections that will require collaboration as we move forward.

[02:33:55] This report covers some initial recommendations and plans for the coming year. A major effort in 2026 will be an online needs assessment survey to be launched in early January. The Commission is especially appreciative of our partnership with George Mason University that helped develop the survey and will assist with data analysis once the survey is completed.

[02:34:23] Our report contains an executive summary as well as greater detail about our work. But briefly, the Commission presents these short-term recommendations. The military presence in Fairfax County and Northern Virginia contributes about 20 percent of the county's economy, although it will take some time to measure and analyze the overall negative effect of recent federal budget cuts and the 43-day government shutdown on Fairfax County, Northern Virginia, and the Commonwealth.

[02:34:54] But keeping skilled military personnel who are transitioning to civilian status is an opportunity for the county to provide career transition support and help in translating military experience into civilian credentialed certifications and jobs locally.

[02:35:13] Working with the Department of Human Resources to expand veteran hiring for county positions and holding periodic military assistance and benefits information sessions also would help keep these skilled workers in the county.

[02:35:29] That was recommendation number one. There are an estimated 12,000 to 14,000 military-connected students in Fairfax County public schools. The school system has committed, as Supervisor Lusk said, to achieving Purple Star designation for all of its schools by the end of the 2026-27 school year.

[02:35:51] It is important for them to meet that deadline. Also, recommendation number two, although I know this is a school issue, delays in transfers of school records can impact appropriate placement in AP and special education programs for children in military families, so extra work to expedite those transfers is imperative.

[02:36:16] Third, child care and spousal employment are additional challenges for military families. Throughout commission discussions, one item arose repeatedly, the need for a county webpage that makes Fairfax County even more welcoming to veterans and military families and ensures that no military family will get lost in Fairfax County.

[02:36:43] The Commission recommends the development of a central information hub located on the county's website designed specifically for active-duty military, veterans, and military families to access pertinent information about county services.

[02:37:02] The Commission looks forward to working with the board and county staff to design, populate, and test such a webpage. I've already referenced the needs assessment survey that will launch in January. The results of that survey likely will form the basis for additional recommendations by the Commission in the coming year.

[02:37:24] But I'd like to offer some comments on a national and local issue that you are all very aware of. As with communities across the nation, affordable housing for veterans and military families is a challenge. Taking a larger percentage of wages, since the basic allowance for housing often does not cover rent and utilities.

[02:37:48] As you know, Fairfax County is an active participant in Built for Zero, a national movement aimed at ending homelessness in communities across the country. Since 2019, Fairfax County has reduced veteran homelessness by more than 40% as part of Built for Zero.

[02:38:06] In FY 2025 alone, Fairfax County ended homelessness for 44 veteran households through the HUD-VASH, Housing Choice Vouchers, SFVF Rapid Rehousing, and other funded housing opportunities. The Commission anticipates that our survey will provide additional insights into housing issues for our veterans and military families.

[02:38:31] Thank you for the confidence you have placed in the Commission as we navigate and explore the unique challenges facing veterans and military families in Fairfax County. Commission members and I are happy to take questions or directions from the board.

[02:39:21] And what a remarkable group of dedicated individuals who, you know, are volunteering to serve on this role. Some retired, some active, but with the same goal, which is to improve the lives of our military-connected families in Fairfax County.

[02:39:39] And just as a reminder, when the board created this commission in 2024, we asked for a clear strategic assessment of the challenges that face our military community, are unique to them. And this report does exactly that.

[02:39:55] I'm struck by some of the things that are challenges all of our community face, but particularly the ones that are acute in our military population. And as I mentioned in the last presentation, growing up near Fort Belvoir and going to school, always with a majority of classmates who are military connected, you can see a lot of the things that Penny just described and the stresses that are unique to that population of public servants.

[02:40:23] And so what we need to be doing and what we want to be doing in Fairfax County is always making sure that we continue to be a place that is a community of choice for our military families and that we are continuing to do. And I think a major theme in this report is streamlining access to services, making it easy for military-connected families to understand how to access certain things.

[02:40:47] And you can imagine, you know, if you're not in a military family that hasn't been transferred somewhere, just imagine how hard it is when any family decides to move from one location to another just to get acclimated. And what we need to be doing is making sure that we have a handout to help them navigate this new community that they're living in here and that we are so proud to have them here in Fairfax County.

[02:41:13] You mentioned the economic impact, defense spending. we know, accounts for 23 percent of the county's economy. And I think that's a huge underestimate because that percent is measured in largely dollars. [02:51:57] The designation of a K&I site highlights FCPD's innovative strategies in establishing these evidence-based practices. NPI conducted an in-depth analysis of our department and included a review of policies, interviews with personnel and participation in scenario-based forums.

[04:11:00] As we stated in our October testimony before the Joint Subcommittee studying this issue in Richmond, no further gaming expansion should occur until such a commission is established and equipped to do its job. [04:11:23] Residents of Fairfax County overwhelmingly oppose a casino, yet proposals continue to service in Richmond driven by the Reston-based developer Comstock Holdings, which has not engaged in good faith with this board or members in the greater Tysons community who would bear the greatest impact of a casino.

[04:12:23] And for decades, Fairfax County has implemented a thoughtful, community-driven vision for Tysons, one centered on livability, economic strength, and high-quality transit-oriented development. A 24-7 casino is fundamentally inconsistent with that vision.

[04:23:08] Without that structure in place, expanding gaming further, especially into newer, more complex forms like online gaming, creates unnecessary risk for our residents and for local governments that will have to deal with the consequences.

[04:23:55] Let's look at a few benefits a moratorium would deliver to Fairfax County. When regulation is fragmented, consumers and communities lose confidence. It becomes harder to protect people from predatory practices, to provide adequate resources for problem gambling, or to hold operators accountable when issues arise.

[04:24:18] A moratorium gives us time to build a system that Virginians can trust. With the Gaming Commission, Virginia could adopt a consistent statewide approach to consumer protection and problem gambling programs modeled on successful systems in other states.

[04:24:36] This pause isn't about opposing gaming. It's about ensuring that when gaming expands, it does so responsibly. A moratorium would give lawmakers, regulators, and localities the breathing room to design evidence-based policies, develop oversight infrastructure, and make sure support systems like addiction services and tax reporting mechanisms are ready before new licenses are approved.

[04:25:07] Introducing new forms of gambling without these safeguards would be like building a house before pouring a foundation. We need to make sure the framework is solid before we add more to it. It's also worth noting that Virginia's Joint Legislative Audit and Review Commission, or JLARC, has already recommended creating a single-state body to regulate gaming, recognizing that it's the best practice nationwide.

[04:25:37] Local officials and community groups across Virginia are calling for the same thing, a temporary pause until this structure is in place. The Reston Citizens Association stands firmly with them. Some might argue that expanding legal gaming helps reduce illegal gambling.

[04:25:58] That's true, but only when the legal market is strongly regulated and transparent. Expanding too quickly before that regulatory framework exists risks making the problem worse, fueling addiction, crime, and community instability, rather than preventing it.

[04:26:16] A gaming commission would help ensure that any future gaming in Virginia is safe, accountable, and beneficial, not harmful or exploitive. In closing, I want to emphasize that this request is about balance and good governance.

Next, we have a public hearing to consider a proposed ordinance to amend the Fairfax County Code by repealing Chapter 28.1 relating to massage therapy, establishments, and services, and adding a new Chapter 28.2 relating to massage establishments, and as soon as they are ready, we will welcome a staff presentation.

[06:11:23] Folks, we're in a board meeting here.

[06:11:55] Staff, welcome. We'd welcome a staff presentation. Chairman McKay, members of the board, my name is Jessica Werder, and I am the Deputy Director of Public Health Operations at the Fairfax County Health Department.

[06:12:11] I am joined here today by Dr. Gloria Adoayensu, who I believe is making her way down, Director of Health, Deputy County Executive Tom Arnold, Peter Sheehan, Division Director for Environmental Health within the Health Department, and John Burton of the County Attorney's Office. As the board is aware, in March of 2020, Supervisors Haraday, Lusk, and Alcorn jointly introduced a board matter related to illicit massage businesses. This board matter directed the county executive to, among other things, conduct a review of possible changes to coordinated revision of the county ordinance regulating massage businesses and relevant county code requirements. The county executive followed up on this request with a memorandum to the board in

[06:12:50] February of 2022 that cited a series of recommendations. These recommendations included strengthening Fairfax County's Massage Ordinance, Chapter 28.1, and a recommendation for the ordinance to reside in an agency with inspection authority, noting that given massage was an allied health profession, oversight of the ordinance should probably reside with the Health Department. We are here today for a public hearing on a new proposed ordinance, Chapter 28.2, known as the Massage Establishments Code. Three versions of this code can be found in the board package as attachments to the board item for this public hearing. Initial work on revisions to the

[06:13:26] county's existing ordinance was informed by inputs from staff in various departments, including the Health Department, the Department of Cable and Consumer Services, the Police Department, Fire and Rescue, the County Attorney's Office, and others. Staff also conducted extensive review of existing massage ordinances in other jurisdictions within Virginia and other states. Staff presented initial concepts for a new revised ordinance in the Board's Public Safety and Security Committee meeting in May of 2025. Following that meeting, staff engaged various stakeholders, including community and industry representatives, for their perspectives on the draft ordinance. In the summer

[06:14:01] of 2025, Health Department hosted six engagement sessions, both in person and virtually, and collected feedback on the draft ordinance from emails, phone calls, and an online form. Staff reviewed over 220 comments on the draft and made several substantive edits to the draft as a result of this input. Presentations were also made to the Consumer Protection Commission and the Healthcare Advisory Board. The result of this process is a recommendation to repeal the existing ordinance chapter 28.1 and as stated previously to adopt a new proposed ordinance 28.2 the massage establishments code revisions are in keeping with the health department's mission to protect promote

[06:14:37] and improve health and well-being for all in our community and in keeping with the interest in strengthening the code in an effort to deter illicit elements within the industry key provisions to note include the elimination of a local permitting requirement for massage therapists transfer of full regulatory authority and oversight from the Department of Cable and Consumer Services to the Health Department, changes to the appeals process for permit revocation, suspension, and denial, introduction of plan review for new and remodeled facilities, introduction of a variance process, updates to the health and sanitation standards, and prohibitions on sleeping quarters, employee use of alcohol or controlled substances, and on sexually

[06:15:14] suggestive advertising. The proposed ordinance maintains the need for an annual permitting of massage establishments and also maintains the requirement for inspection by the Health Department. There is a proposed annual permitting fee of $200 for new establishments and $150 for renewal applications, in addition to a supplemental fee schedule. The board has in front of you today three versions of the draft code, found as attachments one through three to the board item associated with this public hearing. All versions include the provisions I have cited, and all are identical with one exception. Each version takes a different approach to the concept of locked doors

[06:15:48] and controlled entry, which refers to remotely operated unlocking devices or entry systems. These items are hallmarks of the illicit business models the board is interested in deterring. In addition, locked doors and controlled entry systems make inspections challenging and reduce transparency for county staff charged with regulating these establishments.

[06:16:08] Staff's original draft code included prohibitions on controlled entry. That said, this provision has proven to be a particularly challenging provision in conversation with industry representatives, particularly small establishments and sole proprietors, for whom a prohibition on controlled entry and locked doors represents a potential safety risk for their staff and clients. Recognizing this, the three versions attempt to give the board maximum flexibility on this issue. Attachment 1, version 1, prohibits controlled entry outright for all massage establishments, requiring doors to be unlocked during regular business hours, and prohibiting remotely controlled locking devices and entry

[06:16:46] systems attachment two version two is silent on the issue of controlled entry there are no prohibitions and in attachment three version three staff have attempted to strike a balance version three maintains a prohibition a prohibition on controlled entry but grants exemptions to this prohibition for sole proprietors and massage establishments with an unobstructed view into the reception area or waiting area through glass or windows and allows for locked front doors whenever the establishment is occupied by a single employee or independent contractor.

[06:17:20] This is staff's attempt to maintain the intent of prohibiting business models that facilitate illicit activity, while also acknowledging the real concern and feedback from industry partners about safety, primarily with smaller establishments. It should be noted that in all three versions of the ordinance, the option for a variance exists in the code. Finally, I would note a few details found in the enactment clauses of the ordinance. If adopted as written, the board would be authorizing the health department to immediately begin the administrative processes needed to ensure that establishments are permitted by the health department come January 1st,

[06:17:56] 2026, and the board would be authorizing the department to waive permit application fees for applications received prior to January 1st, 2026. This is an attempt to minimize financial burden on establishments that are currently permitted by the Department of Cable and Consumer Services as the proposed ordinance shifts permitting from a rolling cycle to a once-per-calendar year permitting cycle, and we want to eliminate the possibility that any establishment would have to pay twice in the first year of the transition to the health department. [06:27:05] Thank you. Good evening, Chairman McKay, members of the board. I'm Marlene Blum, Chairman of the Health Care Advisory Board. Our complete statement with additional details has been sent to you and is also attached to this testimony. The HCAB recommends that the Board repeal Chapter 28.1 of the Fairfax County Code and adopt the proposed Chapter 28.2, both relating to massage establishments.

[06:27:31] Because providing advice to the Board on health and health-related issues is one of our responsibilities, the Health Care Advisory Board has focused on the important health care and public health components of this new chapter for the County Code. As the Board knows, massage establishments are currently permitted and regulated by the Department of Cable and Consumer Services, but inspected by the Health Department to ensure compliance with health and safety standards.

[06:27:57] The proposed new provisions would transfer full regulatory authority and oversight from DCCS to the Health Department. Adopting this change and the other provisions in the new chapter would demonstrate the Board's recognition of massage therapy as an important allied health profession and would also strengthen community confidence in the safety and professionalism of the county's massage establishments.

[06:28:24] The proposed Chapter 28.2 would eliminate the local permitting requirement for massage therapists because state regulations now require that all practicing massage therapists in Virginia be licensed by the Virginia Board of Nursing.

[06:28:39] It makes sense to remove this provision and instead rely on state licensing, which, according to the American Massage Therapy Association, protects the public through establishing a consistent standard of practice which is enforceable by a professional code of ethics.

[06:28:56] The new chapter requires that those who administer massages in massage establishments be currently licensed by the Virginia Board of Nursing. Turning now to public health, which focuses on preventing problems from happening or recurring, the Health Care Advisory Board feels strongly that health department regulation of massage therapy centers is necessary to protect the public by ensuring a safe, sanitary environment.

[06:29:23] The regulations help prevent the spread of communicable diseases, require that practitioners be properly licensed, trained, and qualified, and help ensure a consistent level of care across different establishments. We not only support these regulations, but we also believe that the responsibility for implementing them should be placed with those who have the necessary expertise, capacity, and experience to do this well.

[06:29:51] In our view, that means the trained professionals in the health department, as proposed. To sum up, the Health Care Advisory Board supports the new regulations because they will help protect and promote the health and safety of the community and because they will help to build trust in the massage therapy profession and confidence in the massage establishments in Fairfax County.

[06:30:52] Tonight, I want to thank you for considering amendments to Chapter 28.1 of the County Code, especially as they relate to illicit massage businesses, or IMBs, which are central to our mission to eradicate.

[06:31:09] IMBs are storefronts where traffickers use fraud, force, and coercion to compel women to provide sexual services under the guise of massage. Many victims are recruited from China, Korea, Vietnam, promise legitimate work only to incur massive debt, debt bondage that becomes the trafficker's leverage of control.

[06:31:33] Here in Fairfax County, Reset 180 has identified 169 suspected illicit massage businesses. This is data based on research, online reviewing, all reviews, but also verified in person by our team.

[06:31:53] Our team has documented consistent indicators of trafficking, including sexually suggestive online advertising, obstructed visibility into the business, heightened exterior security including remote control locks, late night operations far outside legitimate massage hours. Women in these businesses are forced to perform sexual acts six to ten times a day or more. These are not just remote statistics. These are human beings in our community who we need to protect. I want to share

[06:32:30] one story that represents many like her. Zhao Ling, a woman from China, came to our attention last year when Virginia State Police contacted us. She had been arrested while trying to re-enter the illicit massage business where she had been exploited. The landlord had locked her out for unpaid rent. She was held without bond, terrified and alone. But, like our law enforcement officers do such a great job, the arresting officer recognized the indicators of trafficking.

[06:33:01] He contacted Virginia State Police who reached out to us. That one decision changed the course of her life. When we first met her, she was frail, ashamed, overwhelmed. She feared deportation, retaliation, and everyone around her.

[06:33:18] Trust was impossible. Everyone in her life, including her family back in China, and everyone she had known here in the United States, had exploited her. Weeks later, her charges were dropped thanks to a sensitive judge who recognized that she was a victim of trafficking.

[06:33:35] That night, she called our hotline. We met her at 9 p.m., placed her in safe housing. For 15 days, my team provided food, transportation, legal support, clothing, and a Mandarin-speaking volunteer who stayed with her. She told us, No one in my life has ever shown me this much dignity and kindness.

[07:45:27] A 5.5-acre tract is preserved as private open space in the Long Branch Stream Valley on the western edge of the site, and it provides a vegetative buffer between the subject site and suburban neighborhoods with single-family detached townhomes to the west.

[07:46:29] The nominator submitted illustrative concepts showing two scenarios in which the subject site could be consolidated and redeveloped under the proposed plan amendment. [07:46:49] Traditional townhomes, stacked townhomes, and or triplex units would make up the balance of the site. [07:47:12] The subject site is just west of the transit station area, and it's split between three zones of the adjacent to core areas, non-core areas, and the suburban center edges, which are planned to be maintained as a buffer between Merrifield and the adjacent suburban neighborhoods.

[08:10:54] Okay, our final public hearing is a public hearing on proposed plan amendment 2022-CW-2CP, Comprehensive Plan Policy Plan Amendment Phase 1. And as staff is getting ready, I did announce this morning in the first half of the day that we are going to have the public hearing, but we will have a motion to defer decision so the board has time to react.

[08:11:26] So when you are ready. Good evening, members of the board. Thank you. I'm Corrine Bebeck with the Department of Planning and Development. I'm joined by my team here, which includes Kelly Atkinson and Tracy Strunk, also from the Department of Planning and Development, and then our topic leads team, which includes Carly Aubrey, Salem Bush, William Nance, Leah Niebauer, Katrina Knutson, Michael Burton, and Michelle Stahlhut, also from DPD, Anna Ricklin from the Health Department, Bob Pecora and Taylor Wehmeyer from FCDOT, and Ben Zeng from LDS.

[08:12:01] The Board of Supervisors authorized the consideration of a comprehensive plan amendment to update the countywide policy plan. The goals of this update are to streamline the current plan, align with the strategic plan, one Fairfax, and other recently adopted plans, to update policies, and to add new policies.

[08:12:17] Due to the magnitude of the policy plan, the project was divided into two phases, with Phase 1 focusing on the elements most frequently used as part of the development review process. Phase 2 will commence upon the adoption of Phase 1. Beginning in the spring of 2024 and culminating in the spring of 2025, staff conducted extensive outreach through in-person and virtual community meetings, pop-up events, and industry group collaboration, as well as collaborating closely with multiple county agencies and boards.

[08:12:45] The feedback was factored into the initial draft plan text published in the spring of 2025 and refined in the changes published in the staff report. Further collaboration was reflected in the most current draft plan text dated November 7, 2025. The policy plan has strong policies that have provided staff the flexibility to respond to changing conditions and technology over the years.

[08:13:07] As such, the overall changes to the policy plan include streamlining the elements, modernizing language used, alignment with county plans, and incorporation of equity throughout the document. In addition to removing outdated information, we've also added new objectives and policies and incorporated the community feedback we've received.

[08:13:26] The rebranded comprehensive plan overview strengthens connections between the policy plan and the area plans while providing broader context for the county's land use goals. It introduces a contextual land use planning narrative outlining Fairfax County's social and land use evolution, highlighting how population growth and suburbanization have shaped current challenges.

[08:13:47] These challenges serve as the basis for the land use vision and goals, which are closely aligned with the countywide strategic plan to guide strategic planning around housing, the economy, transportation, the environment, equity, and healthy communities. The remainder of the comprehensive plan overview has been updated, but has not changed substantially.

[08:14:05] One notable update was to emphasize a modern, inclusive approach to community engagement, aligning with the countywide strategic plan. While the board direction was to consider a standalone equity element, staff determined that each element plays a role in contributing to equity and equitable development.

[08:14:21] Therefore, an equity objective and associated policies have been provided with each individual element. The new healthy communities element focuses on health priorities related to land use. The element recognizes the role the built environment plays in shaping community health through promoting accessible recreational spaces, safe and healthy housing, active transportation, a sense of community and social connectedness, food security, and ensuring clean air and water quality.

[08:14:49] The policy plan currently contains a human services element that has objectives and policies that are related to health. However, these policies focus on services and service delivery models, many of which are outdated and do not meet current best practices. Relevant objectives and policies from the human services element have been repositioned to this newly created healthy communities element.

[08:15:12] The updated land use element now has a streamlined structure that better reflects the goals of the comprehensive plan overview. Former appendices have been folded into the element, either as policies, use-specific guidance, or for larger topics as dedicated criteria sections.

[08:15:28] This approach simplifies references, improves consistency with broader land use goals, and creates a framework that can more easily adapt to future policy needs and development trends. A number of new and expanded policy areas have been introduced. A new placemaking objective highlights the role of high-quality urban design and community identity in shaping growth.

[08:15:49] The visual and performing arts element is folded into land use, making arts and culture part of the core development review process. Suburban village centers are a new planning concept meant to give more flexibility to commercial areas within suburban neighborhoods and may be well-suited for mixed-use development, including medium-density housing.

[08:16:09] The updated element strengthens the county's commitment to equity and displacement issues. Two new objectives address cultural and economic displacement, calling for thoughtful planning that connects residents to opportunities, enhances community identity, and ensures equitable access to resources.

[08:16:26] Flexibility is added in mixed-use centers, allowing shifts between employment and residential uses without increasing overall density. Policies also reinforce the importance of maintaining a diverse economy, especially by protecting limited industrial land and encouraging commercial uses that provide jobs and services. The plan includes new criteria for office repurposing, offering flexibility to convert older office buildings into housing, permit limited expansions, and repurpose surface parking areas for added residential development. Revised data center criteria builds upon the staff work conducted as

[08:16:59] part of the 2024 data center report and adopted zoning ordinance amendments. The transportation element has been streamlined to reduce and eliminate redundancies, but also to retain the emphasis areas. New policies have been introduced to continually take transportation forward to enhance a multimodal network that will serve the community, offering mobility choices and the services needed to support transportation. Within the existing environment element, it was recognized that there were some redundancies and outdated information, so a big goal of the update was to streamline the existing policies while retaining the existing

[08:17:38] structure. Some notable changes include aligning policies with CCAP, Resilient Fairfax, the operational energy strategy and other documents to better link the importance of these policies to the goals of greenhouse gas emission reduction and creating a more resilient natural and built environment. We've formalized policies that have been successfully implemented through development projects such as the 50% undisturbed open space in the RC zoning district associated with special exception requests and incorporated bird-friendly design and natural landscaping policies. We've enhanced policies to better support site design and emphasize the environment within developments and have drafted countywide stormwater management guidance.

[08:18:19] Some of the rationale that went into the new stormwater guidance was to provide predictability across the county as we currently have over 50 individual stormwater management recommendations within different parts of the comprehensive plan, making it difficult to know what to predict for new development. The guidance is set up in order to provide flexibility and is similar to guidance in other parts of the comprehensive plan, such as Ruston and the Dulles Suburban Center.

[08:18:42] The guidance has generally been included in various SSPAs over the years, so we've formalize the expectation across the county rather than developing it with each individual SSPA nomination or site. The policy guidance is intended to capture projects with higher intensities or densities and impervious coverage where existing county code does not require a substantial improvement and will be most applicable to projects proposed in activity centers where a higher level of intensity or density is planned and where similar language already exists if it recently went through an SSPA process. Lower density developments where existing county code would

[08:19:18] provide sufficient water quantity or quality controls will basically be able to meet the plan guidance simply by meeting code. The purpose of this guidance is to improve watershed and stream health and reduce erosion and flood risk potential. Finally, the goal for phase one of the parks and recreation element has been to incorporate the Fairfax County Park Authority's Parks, Recreation, and Open Space and Access strategy, also referred to as PROSA. PROSA was adopted by the Park Authority Board in fall of 2023 after an extensive two-phase community outreach process. With the four PROSA components in mind, we updated existing policy texts to

[08:19:57] recognize equitable access to parks and recreation services, and we added a new Objective 7, which spells out the PROSA strategy's four components. Additional opportunities to streamline and update the element will be identified and brought forward during Phase 2, and some of this work will be informed by the Park Authority Needs Assessment. Finally, staff recommends that the policy plan be amended to replace the existing preface introduction, land use, transportation, environment, and parks and recreation elements, add a new healthy communities element, remove the existing

[08:20:33] human services and visual and performing arts elements, and update the glossary as shown in the staff revised text dated November 7th, 2025, which supplements the September 10th, 2025 staff report and recommendation. Oh, my mic's not on.

[08:39:19] In particular, I gave comment during the environmental, land use, and healthy community sessions. I have included those comments with my testimony tonight. Your own white paper speaks to the need for clean air. It states human services needs to look beyond traditional human service functions and understand the built environment plays a key role in creating healthy communities. Your paper pulls from the American

[08:39:55] Planning Association Healthy Communities Policy Guide, which offers a broad definition of healthy communities as places where all individuals have access to healthy built natural environments that give them the opportunity to live their fullest. The APA Guide recommends adopting policies that provide all people a healthy environment, especially clean air. It has a domain for environmental that includes air quality as a subdomain. The APA's metrics for planning healthy communities you cite has five specific measures for tracking success in developing healthy

[08:40:33] communities. Environmental is one of them. The objectives of the county's proposed policy plan has eliminated the element of environmental. The most glaring example of poor air quality locally comes from gas stations. EPA has required air capture controls at gas stations, but the controls are to address ozone levels in the atmosphere and not to address local health risks. 10% and more of the fumes escape into the atmosphere as you can smell when filling your tank or when tanker trucks fill the underground tanks. Many others across the country have setbacks to isolate gas stations

[08:41:12] from areas of sensitive use like residential parks and schools. I have raised this health issue risk with the county many times over the last couple of years.
